# Waves, probability and quantum tunnelling

> **Why** can the Sun shine, when its protons are far too slow to reach each other?

<svg data-fig="hero" viewBox="0 0 1200 600" role="img" aria-label="Illustration: dawn over a dark, hilly landscape. A huge orange Sun is rising behind the hills and lights the sky in yellow and pink. High in the sky a round magnifying lens shows a dark close-up of the inside of the Sun: two tiny red particles move towards each other. ## Electrons one at a time {Slits}

Light passing through two narrow slits makes a pattern of bright and dark bands on a screen. Thomas Young showed such **interference** of light around 1801–1803, and it became the classic proof that light is a wave. In 1909 Geoffrey Taylor repeated a two-slit experiment with light so dim that only a few photons were on their way at any moment, and the bands still appeared.

In 1924 the French physicist Louis de Broglie suggested that electrons might be waves too. In 1927 Clinton Davisson and Lester Germer, and independently George Paget Thomson and Alexander Reid, saw electrons **diffract** from crystals, just like waves. Later, Merli, Missiroli and Pozzi (1974) sent electrons through a two-slit device *one at a time*. The figure shows what such an experiment looks like.

<svg data-fig="slits" viewBox="0 0 600 400" role="img" aria-label="Three panels of a detector screen after a double-slit experiment with single electrons, simulated. Left, after 30 electrons: scattered dots with no clear pattern. Middle, after 300 electrons: the dots begin to gather in vertical bands. Right, after 3000 electrons: clear bright bands of many dots with almost empty bands between them, an interference pattern." style="max-width:100%;font-size:24px">

Each electron arrives at one single point, like a particle. But where it arrives is not decided like a bullet's path: after thousands of electrons, the dots form the bands of a wave pattern. At the Fotoni lab visit you can study a double-slit experiment repeated with single photons (unit *Fotoni lab visit: modern physics experiments*).

## Through the wall {Tunnel}

Roll a ball towards a hill. If its kinetic energy is less than the energy needed to reach the top, it always rolls back. In classical physics the region beyond the hill is simply forbidden. A small particle such as an electron behaves differently. Its wave does not stop at the edge of an **energy barrier**: inside the barrier the wave shrinks quickly, and if the barrier is thin, a small wave comes out on the other side. So the particle has a chance of appearing beyond the barrier. This is **quantum tunnelling**.

<svg data-fig="barrier" viewBox="0 0 600 340" role="img" aria-label="Schematic drawing, not to scale. A grey rectangle in the middle is an energy barrier whose top is higher than the particle&#x27;s energy, shown as a dashed line. On the left, a blue wave of large amplitude moves towards the barrier. Inside the barrier the wave does not oscillate but shrinks smoothly. On the right of the barrier a wave with the same wavelength but a much smaller amplitude continues. The square of the amplitude tells the probability of finding the particle." style="max-width:100%;font-size:24px">

How large is the chance? The table gives approximate model values for an electron whose energy is $1\ \text{eV}$ below the top of a barrier, for different barrier widths.

| Barrier width (nm) | Chance of passing |
| --- | --- |
| 0.25 | 7.7 % |
| 0.50 | 0.60 % |
| 0.75 | 0.046 % |
| 1.00 | 0.0035 % |

Tunnelling is not just a theory. In 1928 George Gamow, and independently Ronald Gurney and Edward Condon, explained **alpha decay** with it: an alpha particle escapes from a nucleus by tunnelling through a barrier that it could never climb. The **scanning tunnelling microscope** of Gerd Binnig and Heinrich Rohrer (1981, Nobel Prize 1986) measures the tunnelling current between a sharp tip and a surface and can show single atoms. The flash memory in your phone stores data by moving electrons through thin barriers by tunnelling.

## Key idea

Light and matter are neither ordinary waves nor ordinary particles. This is called **wave–particle duality**. An electron, a proton or a photon is detected at one point, like a particle, but how it travels is described by a wave. In 1926 Max Born proposed what the wave means: the square of its amplitude at a place gives the **probability** of finding the particle there. Quantum physics cannot say where one electron will land, but it predicts the pattern of many very accurately.

Every moving particle has a **de Broglie wavelength**. When the wavelength is similar to the size of an object in its way, such as the distance between atoms or the width of a barrier, the wave nature becomes important: diffraction, interference and tunnelling. For a ball or a car the wavelength is so tiny that we never notice it.

## Formula: the de Broglie wavelength

A particle with the momentum $p = mv$ has the wavelength
```latex
\lambda = \frac{h}{p} = \frac{h}{mv}
```
where $h = 6.626 \cdot 10^{-34}\ \text{J} \cdot \text{s}$. If the kinetic energy $E_\text{k} = \tfrac{1}{2}mv^2$ is known, the momentum is $p = \sqrt{2mE_\text{k}}$. The chance of tunnelling falls very fast, roughly exponentially, when the barrier becomes wider or higher, or when the particle is heavier.

## Tunnelling and the Sun {Sun}

At the centre of the Sun the temperature is about $15.7$ million kelvins. A typical kinetic energy of a particle there is of the order of $k_\text{B}T \approx 1.35\ \text{keV}$. Two protons repel each other, because both are positive. To fuse, they must come within a few femtometres, where the **strong nuclear force** can hold them together. At $2\ \text{fm}$ the electric potential energy of two protons is about $720\ \text{keV}$, more than $500$ times the typical energy. In classical physics a proton with $1.35\ \text{keV}$ turns back at about $1000\ \text{fm}$ and never gets close.

<svg data-fig="sunbarrier" viewBox="0 0 600 440" role="img" aria-label="Graph with logarithmic scales on both axes. Horizontal axis: the distance r between two protons, from 1 to 3000 femtometres. Vertical axis: energy in kiloelectronvolts, from 0.1 to 10 000. A falling straight line is the electric potential energy of the two protons, which grows as they come closer: 1.44 MeV at 1 femtometre, 0.72 MeV at 2 femtometres. A horizontal orange line at 1.35 keV is the typical kinetic energy at the centre of the Sun. It meets the potential energy line at about 1060 femtometres, the closest a proton with this energy could come in classical physics. A shaded strip below 2 femtometres marks where the strong nuclear force can join the protons." style="max-width:100%;font-size:24px">

</svg>

In 1928 Gamow's tunnelling solved the puzzle. A proton's de Broglie wavelength at these energies is hundreds of femtometres, so its wave reaches far into the barrier, and there is a tiny chance that two protons tunnel close enough to fuse. In 1929 Robert Atkinson and Fritz Houtermans used this idea to make the first calculation of fusion in stars. Each meeting has only a tiny chance, but the Sun's core contains an enormous number of protons, so fusion goes on all the time. An average proton in the Sun's core waits about $9$ billion years before it fuses with another proton: this is why the Sun shines steadily for so long. The chapter *The Energy of the Stars* continues this story.

## Worked example

In an electron microscope, electrons are accelerated from rest through a voltage of $100\ \text{V}$. Calculate their de Broglie wavelength and compare it with the distance between atoms in a crystal, about $0.1\text{–}0.3\ \text{nm}$.

<!-- vaihe -->

**Given:** $U = 100\ \text{V}$, electron mass $m = 9.109 \cdot 10^{-31}\ \text{kg}$, charge $e = 1.602 \cdot 10^{-19}\ \text{C}$, $h = 6.626 \cdot 10^{-34}\ \text{J} \cdot \text{s}$. Find the wavelength $\lambda$.

<!-- vaihe -->

**Principle:** The electric field does the work $eU$ on the electron, which becomes its kinetic energy (conservation of energy). The de Broglie wavelength follows from the momentum.

<!-- vaihe -->

**Formula:** Solved in symbols:
```latex
\begin{aligned}
E_\text{k} &= eU \\
p &= \sqrt{2mE_\text{k}} = \sqrt{2meU} \\
\lambda &= \frac{h}{p} = \frac{h}{\sqrt{2meU}}
\end{aligned}
```

<!-- vaihe -->

**Unit conversions:** None are needed: all quantities are in SI units. The answer in metres is changed to nanometres with $1\ \text{nm} = 10^{-9}\ \text{m}$.

<!-- vaihe -->

**Substitution:**
```latex
\begin{aligned}
E_\text{k} &= 1.602 \cdot 10^{-19}\ \text{C} \\
&\quad \cdot 100\ \text{V} \\
&= 1.602 \cdot 10^{-17}\ \text{J} \\[6pt]
2mE_\text{k} &= 2 \cdot 9.109 \cdot 10^{-31}\ \text{kg} \\
&\quad \cdot 1.602 \cdot 10^{-17}\ \text{J} \\
&= 2.919 \cdot 10^{-47}\ \text{kg}^2\text{m}^2/\text{s}^2 \\[6pt]
p &= \sqrt{2.919 \cdot 10^{-47}\ \text{kg}^2\text{m}^2/\text{s}^2} \\
&= 5.402 \cdot 10^{-24}\ \text{kg} \cdot \text{m/s} \\[6pt]
\lambda &= \frac{6.626 \cdot 10^{-34}\ \text{J} \cdot \text{s}}{5.402 \cdot 10^{-24}\ \text{kg} \cdot \text{m/s}} \\
&= 1.227 \cdot 10^{-10}\ \text{m} \\
&= 0.123\ \text{nm}
\end{aligned}
```

<!-- vaihe -->

**Answer:** The electrons have the de Broglie wavelength $\lambda \approx 0.123\ \text{nm}$, three significant figures like the constants. It is of the same size as the distance between atoms in a crystal, so the electrons diffract from crystals, as Davisson and Germer saw.

<!-- vaihe -->

**Reasonableness:** Units: $\text{C} \cdot \text{V} = \text{J} = \text{kg} \cdot \text{m}^2/\text{s}^2$, so the square root gives $\text{kg} \cdot \text{m/s}$, a momentum, and $\text{J} \cdot \text{s} : (\text{kg} \cdot \text{m/s}) = \text{m}$. The speed is $p/m \approx 5.9 \cdot 10^{6}\ \text{m/s}$, about $2\ \%$ of the speed of light, so classical momentum is good enough. The wavelength is much shorter than that of visible light ($400\text{–}750\ \text{nm}$), which is why electron microscopes can see much smaller details than light microscopes.

## Common misconception

"In tunnelling the particle borrows energy to climb over the barrier" or "it finds a hole". Neither is true. The particle's wave reaches through the barrier, and there is a probability of finding the particle on the other side, with the same energy it had before. Most particles still bounce back.

A second mistake is to think that tunnelling happens only in theory, or to big objects too. It is measured every day in microscopes, electronics and radioactive decay, but its chance falls so fast with mass and width that a ball never tunnels through a wall. A third: wave–particle duality does not mean that an electron is sometimes a wave and sometimes a particle. It is always the same quantum object; the wave tells us the probabilities.

## People in this story {People}

### Louis de Broglie (1892–1987)

<!-- photo: louis-de-broglie -->

Louis de Broglie was born in Dieppe, in Normandy, into the noble de Broglie family. He first studied history and took a degree in it in 1910, but then turned to physics. In his doctoral thesis of 1924, *<span lang="fr">Recherches sur la théorie des quanta</span>*, he proposed that every particle, such as an electron, also behaves like a wave. Three years later experiments with crystals confirmed it. He received the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1929 "for his discovery of the wave nature of electrons". His idea is the basis of tunnelling.

## Summary

- Light and matter show **wave–particle duality**: they are detected as particles, but they travel as waves. In the double-slit experiment single electrons build up an interference pattern.
- The wave gives the **probability** of finding the particle (Born, 1926).
- Every particle has a de Broglie wavelength $\lambda = h/p$.
- **Quantum tunnelling**: a particle has a small chance to pass a barrier that it could not climb in classical physics. The chance falls very fast with the width and height of the barrier and the mass of the particle.
- In the Sun, protons with about $1.35\ \text{keV}$ face a barrier of hundreds of $\text{keV}$. Tunnelling, and an enormous number of protons, let fusion happen anyway.
